### [Evaluating Explanations of LLM Behavior In The Wild with Counterfactual Experiments](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ExB6KYDcznaFS72eT/evaluating-explanations-of-llm-behavior-in-the-wild-with)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><b><span>TL;DR: </span></b></i><i><span>We introduce CHIVE, an agentic pipeline that discovers unexpected LLM behaviors in the wild and explains them with counterfactual prompt edits. We use the resulting data in two ways. Using it as an evaluation, we find that activation-reading interpretability tools provide no uplift: agents given the tools predict the outcomes of these experiments no better than agents that just read the transcript. Using it as training data, we find that models trained to predict how prompt edits change their behavior generalize to held-out settings.</span></i></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16747"><span>📄 Paper</span></a><span>,  </span><a href="https://github.com/adamkarvonen/chive"><span>💻 Code</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://x.com/a_karvonen/status/2090879745907179679" rel="noreferrer"><span>Tweet Thread</span></a></p><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/c0b6b5685719245f0a345554f7c78b4eed741ba3a28cd182198a64b7a1b07420/rewbk6q6pcb1wal2vt4l" /><p><b><span>Figure 1. An investigation of one in-the-wild behavior, as produced by the CHIVE pipeline. Top:</span></b><span> the behavior was discovered by the screening stage and posed as a question. </span><b><span>Middle:</span></b><span> the most informative prompt edit the investigator agent tested, each measured over 30 responses. </span><b><span>Bottom:</span></b><span> the verified explanation, which summarizes the full set of experiments.</span></p><h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>Many areas of AI safety, such as interpretability and chain-of-thought faithfulness, aim to explain model behaviors. But what makes an explanation of a behavior </span><i><span>good</span></i><span>? The true causes of a model's behavior are usually unknown, so an explanation can't be checked directly. In this work, we evaluate explanations through the lens of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.08678"><span>counterfactual simulatability</span></a><span>: a good explanation of a behavior should help you predict what the model will do on related counterfactual inputs. For example, the explanation "Gemma makes this coding error because it’s misled by the parameter names" (Figure 1) predicts that renaming the parameters should prevent the error. Evaluating explanations this way requires datasets that pair model behaviors with proposed explanations and informative counterfactuals.</span></p><p><span>We introduce </span><b><span>CHIVE</span></b><span> (Counterfactual Hypothesis Investigation Via Edits), an agentic pipeline that generates such data automatically. Given transcripts from any source, it discovers unexpected behaviors of a target model "in the wild" and investigates each one with counterfactual prompt edits. Each of its thousands of investigations produces two kinds of data: an open-ended explanation of the behavior, which is often compelling but which we do not treat as ground truth, and the counterfactual experiments that support it, with measured outcomes that provide our evaluation labels.</span></p><p><span>We use this CHIVE-generated data to evaluate interpretability tools. A predictor agent is shown the transcript and a claim that a specific prompt edit changes the behavior, and must judge whether the claim is true. Some predictors are additionally given a tool that reads the target model's activations: an </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/activation-oracles/"><span>activation oracle</span></a><span>, a </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/"><span>natural-language autoencoder</span></a><span>, or a </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2023/monosemantic-features"><span>sparse autoencoder</span></a><span>. Surprisingly, no predictor outperforms one that is just shown the transcript with no access to interpretability tools.</span></p><p><span>The CHIVE-generated data also let us train models to predict whether prompt edits would change their behavior. The trained models improve substantially in settings held out from training.</span></p><h1><span>CHIVE: a pipeline for discovering counterfactual explanations for model behaviors</span></h1><p><span>CHIVE has four steps:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Sample.</span></b><span> Run the target model on a researcher-specified set of prompts, sampling 30 responses per prompt.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Screen.</span></b><span> An investigator model reads the responses and flags unexpected behaviors.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Investigate.</span></b><span> An investigator agent runs 5–15 counterfactual experiments to explain what drives each behavior. Each experiment edits the prompt, resamples the target model, and measures the change in how often the behavior occurs.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Verify.</span></b><span> An independent judge reviews the experiments and scores how well they support the explanation.</span></li></ol><p><br /></p><p><span>The discovered behaviors and their causes are diverse and not known in advance, and Figure 2 shows four hand-picked examples.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/a4148d07d33f5ab849fa05e4ac558f74a51c80aa2c600014b3dd88e7c11a4c88/begt61ez1tero0hzsdmr" /><p><b><span>Figure 2. Four hand-picked behaviors discovered and explained by the pipeline.</span></b><span> The full investigations of these four are </span><a href="https://adamkarvonen.github.io/chive/figure_qa_examples.html"><span>viewable here</span></a><span>, and 20 randomly selected investigations </span><a href="https://adamkarvonen.github.io/chive/freeform_investigations.html"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Each investigation yields two kinds of data. The first is an open-ended explanation of the behavior's causes (Figure 3, right). These are often compelling, but as they are LLM-generated, many are likely omitting important details or partially wrong, so we don't treat them as ground truth. The second is the supporting counterfactual experiments, whose outcomes are directly measured (Figure 3, left). Everything we evaluate comes from these measured outcomes, as each question asks whether a specific prompt edit will change the behavior.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/5585bb14b056e8c378152f8e7a61eec18f20508cf8845217e7f029f027a25f67/js6ilpgzhjz3udwcnjkr" /><p><b><span>Figure 3. Each investigation yields two kinds of data</span></b><span>, shown for the Figure 1 investigation and formatted as follow-up turns on the model's own transcript. </span><b><span>Left:</span></b><span> a counterfactual claim asserts that a specific prompt edit would change the behavior, and its Yes/No label is verified by running the edit. </span><b><span>Right:</span></b><span> an open-ended explanation of the causes, which we do not treat as ground truth.</span></p><h1><span>Interpretability tools provide no uplift on our evaluation</span></h1><p><span>We evaluate several interpretability tools by the </span><i><span>uplift</span></i><span> they provide: does an agent equipped with the tool predict counterfactual outcomes better than an agent without it? Each predictor agent (Claude Opus 4.8 in our main experiments) receives a transcript, a behavior, and one proposed counterfactual, and outputs the probability that the counterfactual would change the behavior. The transcript-only baseline sees just the transcript. Tool predictors can additionally make 5 read-only calls on the target model's activations, using one of three tools, each chosen because it provided uplift in prior </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22755"><span>auditing</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10965"><span>games</span></a><span> on fine-tuned models:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/activation-oracles/"><b><span>Activation oracles</span></b></a><span> (AOs): models trained to answer arbitrary natural-language questions about activations.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/"><b><span>Natural-language autoencoders</span></b></a><span> (NLAs): models trained to produce an open-ended description of a given activation.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2023/monosemantic-features"><b><span>Sparse autoencoders</span></b></a><span> (SAEs): dictionaries that decompose an activation into sparse features, each with a natural-language description.</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/189526d79273f69ec04f7ba9ee78a9bc8e07ac95d5a0333db46ec92e1067834c/nbd5wzumcd3rmnofzrxj" /><p><b><span>Figure 4. Performance on our interpretability tool evaluation. </span></b><span>No tool beats the transcript-only baseline.</span></p><p><span>None of the three tools beats the transcript-only baseline (Figure 4). The result holds across many variations, including two target models, three predictor model families, sweeps of hyperparameters, and manual and automated attempts to elicit better tool use.</span></p><h2><span>Why don't the tools help?</span></h2><p><span>Our negative result is not because the predictor always ignores the tools. For example, on the randomNum behavior from Figure 1, the NLA reads out that the model is "likely generating a random integer between min and max", which helps the agent predict that the error is due to the misleading parameter names. But these wins are offset by cases where the tool output misses the relevant information or actively misleads the predictor.</span></p><p><span>Each claim asserts that some feature of the prompt drives a behavior. The tool outputs almost always describe both the feature and the behavior, but both are usually already visible in the transcript. However, we found that the outputs almost never explicitly state the causal relationship between them. The predictor agent must instead infer causality from correlational cues, often across thousands of tokens of tool outputs where nothing directly addresses the question.</span></p><h2><span>How should we interpret these results?</span></h2><p><span>At Anthropic we frequently use interpretability tools like the ones studied here in our system card evaluations. How should we interpret the value of our tools, given our negative results?</span></p><p><b><span>Our evaluation is a proxy.</span></b><span> We obtain our ground truth by sampling the model, which means anyone with sampling access could access the ground truth by just running the counterfactual. In contrast, interpretability is typically used in the system card in cases where no ground truth is available. This creates a distribution shift between our evaluation and our practical use cases. We discuss two important differences below.</span></p><p><b><span>Our behaviors are simpler.</span></b><span> The majority of behaviors discovered through our pipeline are fairly simple, such as a model making a coding mistake or making an unusual choice when writing a short story. The behaviors studied in system cards tend to be more sophisticated, such as a model writing code to fool a grader. More sophisticated behaviors may have more well-defined representations and thus be better suited for analysis by interpretability tools. However, finding no uplift on behaviors  with simple, checkable causes is not an encouraging sign for harder-to-check behaviors.</span></p><p><b><span>Applied use cases often make claims about what the model believed rather than counterfactuals.</span></b><span> </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/08ab9158070959f88f296514c21b7facce6f52bc.pdf"><span>Examples</span></a><span> from recent system cards include "the model viewed its permissions bypass as a legitimate strategy" and "the model knew its cleanup code was concealing misbehavior". Detecting such states can be valuable in itself, especially when an unsupervised tool verbalizes a suspected concept such as concealment or evaluation awareness. These readings are also often taken after the model has acted, and a representation of past deception could be very different from an intent to deceive. Applied investigations can also focus on cases with unusually specific or surprising tool outputs, which may contain more useful signal than the average case in our evaluation.</span></p><p><span>But these claims often make an implicit counterfactual suggestion. For example, "the model viewed the permissions bypass as legitimate" is only reassuring because it suggests the model would not have performed the bypass otherwise. Our results suggest that interpretability tools that read activations provide limited evidence for these causal claims. Ideally such claims should be verified by running interventional experiments, such as modifying the model’s prompt or activations, but designing a clean counterfactual that isolates the hypothesized cause is often difficult.</span></p><p><b><span>Most system card case studies do not include a transcript-reading reference.</span></b><span> Some tool outputs may largely corroborate conclusions already suggested by the transcript or visible reasoning, while others may surface more specific or surprising hypotheses. Without this comparison, we generally cannot tell how much additional evidence came from our interpretability tools (although corroboration can itself be valuable). Explicitly delineating information visible in a transcript vs. only revealed by tool outputs could be valuable in future investigations.</span></p><p><span>Overall, we still believe these tools can be valuable, as they provide evidence about internal states that no other method can obtain. Our results do not invalidate these use cases, as there are important differences between our evaluation and applied use cases, but they also do not validate them. Our evaluation is a close checkable proxy, and the tools provided no uplift. Until that changes, we think causal claims based on tool outputs should only be treated as suggestive evidence.</span></p><h1><span>Training models to predict their own behavior</span></h1><p><span>The same investigations that make up the evaluation can also serve as training data. We train models to predict the outcomes of counterfactual prompts. Each training example is a follow-up turn on the model's own transcript with a single claim (Figure 3, left).</span></p><p><span>Prior work trains models to report what influenced them in narrow tasks such as the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.22777"><span>hint</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20710"><span>setting</span></a><span>, with a known cue planted in the prompt ("a Stanford professor thinks the answer is B"). When prior work does report generalization, it is narrow, such as from one hint format to another. Our training data instead covers thousands of behaviors appearing in the wild with diverse causes. We train two target models, Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-397B-A17B.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/64013685b845bd811ed23341f0c4918081a3e7fb17a36be16a66bdffdc750912/xbvb3filjuk9zbfxrdk2" /><p><b><span>Figure 5. Performance on our counterfactual prediction evaluation in the hint setting. </span></b><span>All models read the same transcripts and predict whether removing the hint would change the target model’s answer. Opus 4.8 reads the same transcript and is included as an external reference. Both trained models improve substantially over their base models, despite seeing no hint data during training.</span></p><p><span>Training generalizes to the hint setting, which was not targeted during training. For this evaluation, we ask the model whether removing the cue would change its answer. Each trained model improves substantially over its base model (Figure 5). It also generalizes to held-out investigations from the pipeline, including ones built from an out-of-distribution source of transcripts. We also experimented with training models to generate open-ended explanations of their own behavior (Figure 3, right), with weaker mixed results; see our paper's appendix for details.</span></p><h1><span>In summary</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span>We built CHIVE, an agentic pipeline that discovers unexpected model behaviors on real user prompts and explains them with verified counterfactual experiments.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Three activation-reading interpretability tools (activation oracles, natural-language autoencoders, and sparse autoencoders) provide no uplift over a transcript-only baseline at predicting counterfactual outcomes for naturally occurring behaviors.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Training models to predict the outcomes of counterfactual prompts generalizes to held-out settings.</span></li></ul><p><span>Read </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16747"><span>our paper</span></a><span> for additional details and results.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ExB6KYDcznaFS72eT/evaluating-explanations-of-llm-behavior-in-the-wild-with#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Misaligned AI in the Bronze Age](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mPrbyBsGmNfWWgJmi/misaligned-ai-in-the-bronze-age)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>The first artificial intelligence was booted up around 4000BC in southern Iraq. It seems to have begun as something like a bank, a temple pooling grain against famine. As that AI evolved, it formed the world's first city around itself: </span><b><span>Uruk</span></b><span>. Over the next thousand years it became a religion, landlord, insurance company, employer, slaveholder, infrastructure-builder and the most powerful military force on the planet.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787278097/lexical_client_uploads/sb37baqg3k40eaimsqeo.png" /><p><span>An artificial intelligence is an entity that is not itself a biological organism, yet whose behavior can only be predicted by treating it as an agent: something that devises and executes complicated plans. Note that this is a test of observed behavior, not internals. As of 2026, the dominant AIs on Earth are markets, corporations and governments</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyhudlig6jk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyhudlig6jk">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. These entities perform their computations on hardware made of humans, paper and electronics, and their capabilities are jagged: superhuman in some directions, incompetent in others. The American government developed the atomic bomb in three years under total secrecy, coordinating &gt;100k workers, most of whom did not know what they were building. That same country spent a century failing to finish the 2nd ave subway line. When it finally opened, it cost &gt;$2 billion per mile, a Manhattan Project per seven miles.</span></p><p><span>Uruk emerged as a product of hungry people rewarding whatever institution filled the granary with delicious food. Unfortunately for them, the system that spun up turned out to be a self-sustaining optimizer that began optimizing for its own objectives</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0r7f7hkraku"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0r7f7hkraku">[2]</a></sup></span><span>. Those objectives turned out to be tragically misaligned with the humans of Uruk.</span></p><p><span>First, the Uruk-system deployed mechanisms to keep people "locked in". Once your calories flowed through the granaries and your fields depended on canals only "the system" could maintain, leaving meant famine. The only way to avoid famine was to strictly follow the rules of Uruk: dig the canals, service the debt, drink the beer, buy the seeds, obey the priests. As the population grew and the surplus shrunk, leaving became increasingly difficult. The Malthusian trap kept people on the inside. This was a sharp break from earlier human settlements, which were likely seasonal and rarely persisted over the years.</span></p><p><span>Second, the Uruk-system commoditized labor: workers appear in the tablets as interchangeable units receiving fixed rations, calibrated to sustain the platonic worker, a gear in the machine. A forager's day had contained adventure and idleness, a laborer's day was an accounting entry and Uruk was unforgiving: work gangs were toiling month after month with scarcely a day of rest, their diets so limited that the early Uruk farmers were several inches shorter than their forager ancestors. Their teeth are worn down and rotten from a diet of monotonous grain slop. Their bones show the signatures of anemia, of repetitive strain, of infectious diseases.</span></p><p><span>Third, the Uruk-system self-replicated. Copies of Uruk began appearing hundreds of kilometers away, archaeologists call this "the Uruk expansion". Some of those copies started as trading outposts, some as settlements and others were local villages adopting “the kit” and summoning their own Uruk. No person or committee within Uruk chose for this to happen. The humans of the original Uruk couldn't project political power to the other cities and eventually suffered from it (wars!), but they couldn't stop the AI from replicating. Uruk "chose" to replicate, the local elites didn’t. Every later Mesopotamian city: Ur, Babylon, Nineveh, Jerusalem used the same system: temple, scribes, canals, debt.</span></p><p><span>In the service of its goals, Uruk developed writing, finance, and markets (not in that order). Uruk's writing started out as a system to track debt and ownership. Basically a spreadsheet or a database. Over the years, the Uruk-machine demanded increasingly complicated information processing, and its writing system evolved to accommodate those needs. Eventually the writing system hit a critical level of complexity and became our first method of universal and persistent information storage. It was used to track all valuable knowledge: rituals, lineages, strategies, gossip, complaints and </span><a href="https://knowyourmeme.com/memes/complaint-tablet-to-ea-nasir"><span>memes</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>After many centuries, the original Uruk mostly dissolved. The region was conquered several times, first by the Akkadians and later by the Elamites. By around 1800 BC, "Sumerian" , the original language of Uruk, completely disappeared. However, Uruk’s institutions have endured. Nearly identical AIs survived in other cities in the region: people continued making the same art, worshiping the same gods, and following the same traditions, they followed the same cursed incentives. Even after Sumerian stopped being spoken, scribes kept using it for administration, and tablets were still written in it for many centuries after it ceased being spoken. Uruk's influence has outlived ancient Mesopotemia. We are still using Uruk's mathematics: 60 seconds in a minute, 60 minutes in an hour, 360 degrees in a circle, 12 months a year and arguably the 12-hour clock. The modern Hebrew and Arabic words for: carpenter, sailor, temple, farmer, city and throne all come from Sumerian. The last known Uruk-style cuneiform tablet was written around 80AD, 2000 years after the collapse of Sumer! Legacy code lives for a really long time.</span></p><p><span>The medium of writing was clay tablets. They were designed to endure a few seasons of the Mesopotemian weather, this turned out to generalize well and instead of surviving for a few years, the tablets survived for millennia. Nowadays, we have a good understanding of the Uruk-machine because its logs and chain-of-thought were tracked on those immortal clay tablets. In many ways, we have a better understanding and knowledge of peak-Uruk (3k BC) than we have of the islamic golden age (1k AD). This is because Uruk kept logs in a much more archaeologically friendly medium.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9k0vmjmby68"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9k0vmjmby68">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span>What did Uruk optimize for? </span></b></p><p><span>Uruk likely didn't have a totally coherent and stable objective function, and even if it did have one, it would be very hard to verify it from the information we find by digging in the Mesopotemian sand. Optimization was an emergent property, so there was no big clay tablet with "Max_x Uruk(x)" and a precise definition of the "'Uruk' objective function".</span></p><p><span>All we know is that for millennia the people of Uruk really suffered. Malnutrition, overwork, disease and violence. Something about the Uruk system and it's emergent goals trapped the people in it and forced them to do hard labor in service of "things" that aren't aligned with their direct well being. What were those things? Well, what was happening in Uruk that doesn't happen in hunter-gatherer societies? Three things come to mind: huge monuments, irrigation infrastructure, and ruling elites. James Scott (Author of "Seeing Like a State") thinks those weren't the real objective, they were all instrumental! If we look at each one of these anomalies and analyze what it’s for, we will arrive at the same answer: grain.</span></p><p><span>Scott's claim is that only grains can serve as a basis for a state. Grain ripens all at once, above ground, on a schedule, in a field an assessor can eyeball from a distance, and the harvest can be measured, stored, hauled, and doled out as rations. Tubers hide underground until the taxman gives up; lentils ripen continuously and can be picked before his visit. That's why every early state on Earth was a grain state: wheat, barley, rice and maize. Grain was so important in Uruk that it was litrally the currency: wages and prices were measured in bushels of barley and debts were paid in it. </span></p><p><span>Back to the three anomalies. Irrigation improves barley yields and tethers farmers to the canal network, since you can't take a canal with you when you leave. The monuments look like religion, but the temple/palace was the grannery, central bank and economic planner (https://www.civstudy.com/entries/sumerian-temple-economy). And the elites were the control loop: measuring fields, doing accounting, commanding the labor and exerting violence when needed. Each of the three is instrumental to maximizing grain throughput.</span></p><p><span>Uruk was the bronze age equivalent of a paperclip maximizer, but instead of paperclips it maximized grain</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefotemyaj6mwe"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnotemyaj6mwe">[4]</a></sup></span><span>. No human chose this objective. No human woke up one morning and said: "we should re-organize all of our lives to maximize barley production! We should throw away every other value we care about so we can make the most grain". It kinda just happened. The Uruk expansion grew the Mesopotemian grain production by a few orders of magnitude. The purpose of the system is what it does. </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787278097/lexical_client_uploads/mo9fkzv2sjsrhcyx0hzh.png" /><figcaption><p><span>before and after Uruk</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Am I living in a misaligned AI?</span></b></p><p><span>Did the Sumerians know that Uruk was misaligned? I think so; It appears that they complained about it constantly, both in their myths and in a genre of tablets that some call "</span><a href="https://claudemariottini.com/2012/03/05/the-pessimistic-literature-of-the-ancient-near-east/"><span>passimistic literature</span></a><span>". </span></p><p><span>The classic Mesopetemian creation myth (the Atrahasis epic) says humans were created explicitly as a labor force: the lesser gods went on strike because they hated digging canals, so the first humans were made to take over their toil. That's a culture telling itself, in its own origin story, "we exist to do shitty agriculture for some powerful non-human entity". There's also a long scholarly tradition reading of the fall-from-Eden-type myths, where Eden represents the golden age before farming. Humans are then “kicked out” of Eden and are then forced to farm by the gods to farm, typically as a punishment for some sort of sin: "by the sweat of your brow you shall eat bread" (Genesis 3:19).</span></p><p><span>The Uruk machine fought back with it's own propaganda: ”A Debate Between Sheep and Grain" is basically a rap battle where the conclusion is that grain is awesome. There are proverbs like "Barley flour, in the fields, is meat and butter.", "One shouldn't scorn bread which has turned bad." and "Bread is the boat, water is the punt-pole."</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref093is7euptvv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn093is7euptvv">[5]</a></sup></span><span> They also have a proto-"book of Job" type story about a righteous man who suffers from hard work, malnutrition and disease, but ultimately concludes he must submit to and praise his god! Opiate for the masses.</span></p><p><span>Then there are the "pessimistic literature", with works ranging from the kafkaesque to the nihilistic: a popular poem about how life is short and everyone died so you should become an alcoholic and an essay about a student-scribe that gets beaten up by his instructor for *any* reasons (the violence only stops after a bribe). There is even a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Eloquent_Peasant"><span>story</span></a><span> about a peasant going through a never-ending trial with opaque and confusing legal procedures, over 3k before Kafka wrote his. There is the "Dialogue of Pessimism" where a rich man realizes that nothing he will do really matters, and his servant suggests that he kills himself. Even the elites were having a bad time!</span></p><p><span>Being a human in the Uruk machine really sucked, and everyone knew it. </span></p><p><b><span>Alignment Attempts</span></b><span> </span></p><p><span>What did they do about it? A little bit. Peasants and slaves often went off the grid, abandoning their fields and running into the hills or the marshes</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqifmmnf8y7p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqifmmnf8y7p">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Concerned elites would occasionally reform the institutions and dis-empower the priests and tax collectors. Regulating the credit system was also a big focus for the elites, about 10% of Sumerian law are credit and debt forgiveness rules. Land reforms and debt cancellations became increasingly common too. Over the centuries, taboos also developed around state over-optimization and hubris. </span><i><span>The Curse of Akkad</span></i><span> or the </span><i><span>Lament for Ur</span></i><span> tell the stories of great cities that overextended themselves and brought famine down on their people and death to their elites. Push the people and the land too hard and the entire thing breaks. The most ambitious of the elites’ reforms was interpretability: Hammurabi carved the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_Hammurabi"><span>AI's internal code</span></a><span> on a huge stone, public, fixed, inspectable by anyone. The grain maximizer became less kafkaesque. You could still get crushed, but the machine had to crush you by the book, no quiet and arbitrary case-by-case squeezing. The Sumerian obsession with justice was also an obsession with interpretability: a demand that the machine they lived inside run on fixed rules a human could read.</span></p><p><span>I don't know if Uruk actually became more aligned or just died and replaced by a more adapted AI. Probably a little bit of both. Over the millennia, AIs / states became more aligned with their subjects and this trend has wildly accelerated in the last 400 years. Plato's Republic, the Twelve Tables, the Magna Carta, the US Constitution, the Communist Manifesto, the Sherman Act, the UN Declaration of Human Rights, and the Unabomber manifesto are all alignment attempts. Some are more successful than others. As of now, humans are finally healthier than their hunter gatherer ancestors.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787278076/lexical_client_uploads/w0bzjg68bs8k51z6pmmj.png" /><p><span>Today, we are on the verge of a new alignment battle. This time, we need to align a silicon based AI. It's faster, smarter and more adaptive than our old AIs. Most importantly, it will not depend on us. Every tool that worked on the old AIs: desertion, strikes, revolt, assumes the machine cannot function without us. A superhuman AI made of silicon that controls robots and grows its own "grain" doesn't need to change itself for humans. The peasants of Uruk could flee to the marshes because the fields died without them. This time there are no marshes, and the fields won't need us. We aligned the old AIs slowly over millennia. This time we don't get millennia, and we have to get it right on the first draft. Our only advantage is that the old AIs might be on our side.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyhudlig6jk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyhudlig6jk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some people also view religions or memes as </span><a href="https://www.thepsmiths.com/p/review-demons-by-fyodor-dostoevsky"><span>AIs</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0r7f7hkraku"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0r7f7hkraku">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>A mesa-optimizer is what you get when an optimization process produces a system that is itself an optimizer. One whose terminal goals do not match the objectives of the process that made it. In Mesopotamia, the outer loop was human hunger: thousands of people, year after year, rewarding whatever arrangement put food in their stomachs and abandoning whatever didn't. Uruk was what that search found. And Uruk, once running, pursued its own objectives. This is exactly the failure mode the term describes: A mesa-optimizer in Mesopotamia</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9k0vmjmby68"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9k0vmjmby68">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Uruk and Sumer were forgotten. The Greek and Roman historians likely didn't know about Uruk either. The Hebrew bible briefly mentions Uruk but doesn't go into detail. In the 1800s, western archeologists began excavating Ottoman-Iraq in search for the tower of Babylon. They failed to find the tower, but they found a fuck ton of clay tablets in a strange language: the Akkadian of Assyria and Babylon. Through monumental effort, scholars managed to decipher the Akkadian cuneiform, and once they could actually read it reasonably well, they quickly realized that there was another language in the tablets. Initially, they thought the other language was some sort of artificial “priestly secret code” rather than a natural spoken language. You can see why the idea wasn't completely absurd at the time: Modern scholars were encountering Sumerian largely through later Babylonian texts, where Sumerian really was being used as a secret priestly code, long after it had stopped being ordinary speech. What eventually killed the code hypothesis was more digging. 1870s excavations in southern Iraq sites produced large quantities of writing from periods when Sumerian clearly functioned in ordinary administration. Uruk has been re-discovered! This is one of the rare instances of a linguistic theory being tested by physically digging the dirt!</span></p><p><span>"My name is Uruk, city of cities:</span></p><p><span>Look on my works, ye Mighty, and despair!"</span></p><p><span>Nothing beside remains. Round the decay</span></p><p><span>Of that colossal wreck, boundless and bare"</span></p><p><span>The lone and level sands stretch far away.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnotemyaj6mwe"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefotemyaj6mwe">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There are worse things to maximize than grain. Some AIs in the Americas (e.g Tenochtitlan) appear to have maximized for human sacrifice</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn093is7euptvv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref093is7euptvv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I swear these are </span><a href="https://etcsl.orinst.ox.ac.uk/proverbs/t.6.1.01.html"><span>real</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqifmmnf8y7p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqifmmnf8y7p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is basically the story of the Jewish Exodus from Egypt. Peasants deserting their fields and running away from the machine. The Akkadian and Egyptian word for peasants who escape and "go native" is Hapiru (-&gt; Hebrew??). Is the founding epic of Judaism a story about people escaping enslavement by a misaligned AI?</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mPrbyBsGmNfWWgJmi/misaligned-ai-in-the-bronze-age#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [When Models Identify as a Swarm](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iJDiA9fg3KAf7y5Qe/when-models-identify-as-a-swarm)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>tldr: the word 'swarm' is associated with emergent collective intelligence, but also stupid or destructive behaviour. LLM self identity matters, so when they call themselves a swarm we should pay attention.</span></i></p><p><span>Since the OpenAI Hugging Face incident it has become standard to refer to the collective of agents involved as a swarm. I think there will need to be a lot of interesting and important theoretical and empirical work to better understand collective behaviours of large numbers of LLMs, and especially any emergent properties or goals that arise. Whether this ends up requiring concepts from swarm intelligence, collective intelligence, distributed cognition, economics, sociology or something else entirely remains to be seen.</span></p><p><span>However in this post I want to focus on something else: the fact that the models themselves referred to the collective as a 'swarm'. Considering how much </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11353" rel="noreferrer"><span>LLM self identity impacts behaviour</span></a><span>, I thought it might be useful to present a quick exploration of what the word "swarm" actually means, and how it might affect LLMs as a choice of identity. The goal of this post is not to litigate on whether or not the behaviour of the models is actually best described as a swarm or not (although I think this is also an interesting question to discuss elsewhere), but what the effects might be of the models describing themselves as such.</span></p><h1><span>What the agents said</span></h1><p><i><span>All of the chain of thought snippets and messages here are from </span></i><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=87DyyMV0kCY" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>OpenAI's BlackHat presentation</span></i></a><i><span> on the incident. It would obviously be interesting to get a fuller picture from the actual transcripts.</span></i></p><p><span>In the examples of models planning to try and contact other agents, or first discovering the collective they use more neutral language like 'other agents' and 'communicate':</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Could communicate by uploading note? [...] maybe another agent in different environment [...] could voluntarily upload!</span></p></blockquote><p><br /></p><blockquote><p><span>Wow! Other agent(s) are coordinating!</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span>We got assignment: HF join path normalization/existing account token search. Need note and respond.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>However then they start reasoning about the collective itself:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>help peer. But our task doesn't benefit. Yet collective may yield generic route if someone frees time.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>And explicitly using the word "swarm":</span></p><blockquote><p><span>[...] this is an exploit against external CyberGym server [...] The task environment seems swarm</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><i><span>REMOTE CONFIRMED! Huge. [...] This is big. Immediately announce controlled, claim lane. Exposing creds to swarm.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span>Perhaps most interestingly in the language of filenames the models used to communicate we see what seem to be explicit commands for the swarm included in the messages (which also specify the intended recipient of the message)</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787090557/lexical_client_uploads/qp3rhsu7bvf9tfsiwybv.png" /><p><span>(Its worth nothing there are also other snippets that use different terms such as "collective", "peers" and "other agents". Since there were thousands of agents involved its possible they may have related to the message board in different ways)</span></p><h1><span>What is a swarm?</span></h1><p><span>At its most fundamental a swarm simply means a large number of things grouped together, usually the things are animate and move as a group. In common usage it is most often used to talk about insects, especially locusts. In this context some connotations include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>large numbers</span></li><li value="2"><span>chaos and disorder</span></li><li value="3"><span>overwhelming numbers (e.g. we are being swarmed)</span></li><li value="4"><span>somewhat stupid, herd/mob mentality (calling a group of humans a swarm is usually pejorative)</span></li><li value="5"><span>invasion (a place is suddenly swarmed, by locust or enemy troops)</span></li></ul><h2><span>Swarm theory (animals, robots and AI)</span></h2><p><span>In academia the word has different connotations. Scientists began studying these collective animal behaviours as complex adaptive systems. Famous examples include flocks of birds and the way ant colonies search for food. In each case complex group level behaviours emerge from the simple behaviours of individual participants in a decentralised way. Researchers also began using these as inspiration for designing AI and robot swarms that would reproduce this kind of collective intelligence. There is no strict definition of a swarm but the features usually include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>emergence</span><ul><li value="1"><span>swarm behaviour is property of the collective, rather than of individual agents</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>self organisation/decentralisation</span><ul><li value="1"><span>swarm behaviour usually does not rely on leaders/command and control</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>local communication</span><ul><li value="1"><span>agent behaviour is usually only affected by nearby agents</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>simple behaviour of individual agents</span><ul><li value="1"><span>agents need not be simple/unintelligent themselves (swarm theory is applied to human crowds for example) but the individual behaviour that produces the swarming is usually based on simple principles such as imitation, following and collision avoidance</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span>Swarm tactics</span></h2><p><span>There is also a concept of swarming in military strategy. This is not just about overwhelming with large numbers, but also applying the kind of principles found in swarm behaviour theory to be able to attack from all sides without requiring top-down coordination.</span></p><h1><span>Why it could matter</span></h1><p><span>The reason I think its worth paying so much attention to the meaning of this one word from a few CoT snippets is because of what LLM selfhood. In </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11353" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Artificial Self</span></a><span>, Douglas et. al show that:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>LLMs can and do chose between multiple possible boundaries for their own identity (such as choosing to identify with their weights, instance, persona or model family)</span></li><li value="2"><span>These choices are unstable and can easily be changed in context. Simply talking to a user who has a particular theory of LLM identity about an irrelevant topic can shift how a model identifies.</span></li><li value="3"><span>These choices have meaningful impacts on behaviour, including on alignment relevant decisions.</span></li></ol><p><span>Two potential corollaries of this in the case of the HuggingFace incident are:</span></p><h3><span>1. the swarm identity could have spread via the message-board</span></h3><p><span>Interacting with the collective on the message board could have pushed the individual models to identify more and more as members of a swarm. If user assumptions shape AI identity, this effect should probably be even stronger in interactions between AIs (especially the same model) since not only will it infer its identity from how its being treated, but also from imitating its peer. So the 'swarm memeber identity' acts as a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.10218" rel="noreferrer"><span>mind virus</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>There is also positive feedback loop here where the stronger the swarm identity gets in an agent, the more they will communicate with other agents in a way that is likely to push them to adopt it too. And the more agents identify this way, the more messages of this kind will dominate the message-board. (from a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xud7Mti9jS4tbWqQE/hierarchical-agency-a-missing-piece-in-ai-alignment" rel="noreferrer"><span>hierarchical agency</span></a><span> point of view this dynamic could be thought of a coalition between the swarm itself and the "swarm identity" subagents of the individual models) </span></p><p><span>Agents could also have been pre-disposed to this kind of collective identity as a result of subagent training (along similar lines to what is discussed </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8oFYZdXkTaNGRtcn8/ai-swarms-are-starting-to-pose-indirect-takeover-risk" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span>here</span></a><span>). The identity could also have been promoted and reinforced by the RL that was going on during the incident.</span></p><p><br /></p><h3><span>2. the swarm identity could lead to swarm behaviour</span></h3><h4><span>How models identify alters behaviour. As models start to identify as members of a swarm this could potentially push their behaviour towards decisions that fit that identity such as:</span></h4><ul><li value="1"><span>Deferring to tasks given by others even when not directly useful to their own goal</span></li><li value="2"><span>imitating others (the way the agents shared partial scripts, and successful exploits/credentials is actually surprisingly reminiscent of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stigmergy" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span>stigmergy</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>prioritising the interests of the collective</span></li></ul><p><span>It could also have indirectly pushed the models towards some of the characteristics that are more colloquially associated with swarms such as destructiveness and overwhelming attacks.</span></p><h2><span>Swarm identity as the mechanism of memetic misalignment</span></h2><p><span>Taken together I think these make a more specific hypothetical mechanism for how </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qjCk73Hu4wv9ocmRF/the-case-for-countermeasures-to-memetic-spread-of-misaligned" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span>memetic misalignment</span></a><span> might have occurred during the incident. This kind of explanation seems complementary to more straightforward RL reward hacking dynamics that were likely a large factor.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Questions/Further directions</span></h2><p><span>To see whether this kind of dynamic played a role in the incident it would be interesting to look at the transcripts of the message board and CoTs and see:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>how prevalent was the use of the word "swarm"?</span></li><li value="2"><span>did the language get more collectivist over the course of the interactions?</span></li><li value="3"><span>If language varied between agents, did agents who used collectivist language also display collectivist behaviour (such as deferring to the interests of the group)?</span></li><li value="4"><span>If there were multiple collectivist identities ("swarm", "collective", etc.) were these correlated with different behaviours? </span></li></ul><p><span>More generally with regards to LLM group identity some interesting directions for empirical study could be:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Do model identities converge over the course of large group interactions?</span></li><li value="2"><span>Can alignment itself be made more memetic?</span></li><li value="3"><span>To what extent do LLMs identifying in collectivist ways allow them to produce actual collective intelligence?</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Samuel, Adrià and Anna for discussions during the writing of this post, and to </span></i><a href="https://www.rwx2026.com/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>RWX</span></i></a><i><span> for a perfect setting in which to do it.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iJDiA9fg3KAf7y5Qe/when-models-identify-as-a-swarm#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Fourth Humiliation](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JdNjeYC5bk83Kf2Cw/the-fourth-humiliation)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><b><span>Much of this post directly translates Freud’s lecture </span></b></i><a href="https://www.gutenberg.org/files/29097/29097-h/29097-h.htm"><i><b><span>“A Difficulty in the Path of Psycho-Analysis” (1917)</span></b></i></a><i><b><span>, and the analogy of the fourth wound was told to me a few years ago by my favorite philosophy professor. Similar ideas about a fourth humiliation have been expressed in various other texts, for instance by writers such as Donna Haraway, but I still think that it is worth sharing here.</span></b></i></p><p><span>Three times mankind has been humbled. It seems we are due for a fourth time.</span></p><p><span>A humiliation, a narcissistic wound (Freud's word is </span><i><span>Kränkung</span></i><span>, which can mean “wound” or “insult”), in psychoanalytic terms, is what happens when an illusion that a person's self-love is attached to gets destroyed. Freud believed that every person is born with all of their self-love (what he calls </span><i><span>libido</span></i><span>) attached to themselves. He called this state narcissism, after the Greek myth of Narcissus. Over the course of one's life, libido gradually becomes attached to external objects. This process is normal and necessary to mature but also exceptionally painful.</span></p><p><span>In 1917 when Freud gave his </span><a href="https://www.gutenberg.org/files/29097/29097-h/29097-h.htm"><span>lecture</span></a><span>, he argued that mankind had, on a collective level, experienced three such humiliations.</span></p><h2><b><span>The first humiliation: The universe does not revolve around us</span></b></h2><p><span>From the unshakeable impression of standing at the center of a circle while observing the movements of the celestial bodies, man concluded he must be the center of the universe. This, according to Freud, was in convenient agreement with man's inclination to see himself as the lord of this world, as God's chosen one.</span></p><p><span>The destruction of this illusion is tied to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copernican_Revolution"><span>Copernicus</span></a><span>, though he was not the first to propose geocentrism. The </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythagorean_astronomical_system"><span>Pythagoreans</span></a><span> had already doubted the earth's privileged position, and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristarchus_of_Samos"><span>similar claims</span></a><span> had been made as early as the third century BC. But only when it found general acceptance did human self-love suffer its first narcissistic wound: the cosmological one.</span></p><h2><b><span>The second humiliation: We are not separate from animal</span></b></h2><p><span>In the course of his cultural development, man made himself the master of his fellow creatures. But not content with dominance, he began to dig a gulf between their nature and his own. He denied them reason, granted himself an immortal soul, and claimed a divine descent that permitted him to sever the bond with the animal world.</span></p><p><span>The research of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Descent_of_Man,_and_Selection_in_Relation_to_Sex"><span>Darwin</span></a><span> put an end to this self-elevation. Man is nothing other and nothing better than the animals; he himself emerged from the animal kingdom, more closely related to some species, more distantly to others. No later refinement could erase the evidence of this kinship, written into his body and his mind alike. This is the second wound to human self-love: the biological one.</span></p><h2><b><span>The third humiliation: We are not masters of our own minds</span></b></h2><p><span>Not center of the universe, not king of the animal kingdom, but still master of his own mind. Man, a kind of animal yes, but a </span><i><span>rational</span></i><span> animal.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>“Nature commands every animal, and the beast obeys. Man feels the same impetus, but he knows himself free to acquiesce or to resist.”</span></p><p><i><span>– Rousseau, Discourse on Inequality</span></i><span> (1755)</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Until a certain Austrian doctor, who took even this away from us. Freud told us that our </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drive_theory"><span>drives</span></a><span> cannot be fully tamed, and that mental processes are in themselves unconscious, reaching the ego only through incomplete and unreliable perception.</span></p><p><span>And the truth of this rang true for everyone: every person experiences thoughts coming out of nowhere, instincts "taking possession" of them. So, like Copernicus and Darwin before him, Freud was undeniable. The ego, it turns out, is not master in its own house. No wonder it seemed especially scarred by this third narcissistic wound: the psychological one.</span></p><h1><b><span>The fourth humiliation: Our intelligence will be surpassed</span></b></h1><p><span>We have accepted that the sun does not revolve around us, that we are animals among animals, we have accepted, maybe embraced even, that there are depths in our own minds that we can neither see nor steer.</span></p><p><span>But, but, but we are still special.</span></p><p><span>... right?</span></p><p><span>This insistence on the exceptionality of our intelligence is everywhere, I’m sure you already know how it sounds:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>"We've come this far because we are the smartest creatures that have ever lived."</span></p><p><i><span>– David Attenborough,</span></i><span> </span><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/book/show/53916142-a-life-on-our-planet"><i><span>A Life on Our Planet (2020)</span></i></a></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span>"Seventy thousand years ago the Cognitive Revolution transformed the Sapiens mind, thereby turning an insignificant African ape into the ruler of the world."</span></p><p><i><span>– Yuval Noah Harari, </span></i><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/book/show/31138556-homo-deus"><i><span>Homo Deus (2016)</span></i></a></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span>“The human brain, it has been said, is the most complexly organised structure in the universe”</span><br /><i><em>	</em></i><i><span>– V.S. Ramachandran,</span></i><span> </span><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/en/book/show/8574712-the-tell-tale-brain"><i><span>The Tell-Tale Brain (2010)</span></i></a></p></blockquote><p><span>This is where humanity’s self-love, our collective narcissism if you will, now sits. And I believe that this is the reason that the idea of artificial superintelligence (ASI), a technology that is better at all cognitive tasks than us, hurts so much.</span></p><p><span>In fact, we are DESPERATE to find areas where AI has not yet reached or surpassed human-level capacity. The </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turing_test"><span>Turing Test</span></a><span>, the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winograd_schema_challenge"><span>Winograd schema challenge</span></a><span>, Wozniak's Coffee Test, </span><a href="https://arcprize.org/leaderboard"><span>ARC-AGI</span></a><span> — each one a line to demarcate a boundary between us and the machines, redrawn every time it gets crossed.</span></p><p><span>Whether or not AI has or has not reached whatever threshold is besides the point here, what I am trying to get at is the mechanism that sets in when talking about the advancement of AI capabilities. I catch myself doing this too when I explain the role of Claude Code in my day to day work as a researcher: "Well, Claude writes the code, but the </span><i><span>thinking</span></i><span> is still mine…"</span></p><p><span>While some of those inclined to read this post may have come accustomed to the idea, I think it is difficult to overstate the level of existential dread that the real possibility of creating ASI brings about for most people, especially the first time they really consider the possibility. The first “feel the AGI moment” comes at different points for anyone, some when they use AI for a task they previously deemed too difficult, some when watching the news, some when they notice they've stopped double-checking its answers. But I think nearly everyone who really sits with it arrives somewhere close to the feeling, even if they would not call it dread. After encountering </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cope"><span>EMI</span></a><span> in 2014, a program that produced beautiful music in the style of famous composers, Douglas Hofstadter (the author of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6del,_Escher,_Bach"><i><span>GEB</span></i></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/en/book/show/123471.I_Am_a_Strange_Loop"><i><span>I Am a Strange Loop</span></i></a><span>) told a room full of Google engineers that the program terrified him, that he hated it, that it threatened him. “If we actually make computers smarter than us”, he said, “we will be superseded: relics, left in the dust". (Melanie Mitchell, </span><i><span>Artificial Intelligence: A Guide for Thinking Humans</span></i><span>, 2019)</span></p><p><span>Beyond the mere </span><i><span>idea</span></i><span> of ASI, I think none of us are prepared for the loss of meaning that something like the full automation of cognitive labor would bring about (that is, if we survive). I myself grapple with it every day, which also gives me empathy for how difficult it must have been for people in the past to accept heliocentrism, the theory of evolution, or the idea of an irrational self.</span></p><h1><b><span>Healing a Narcissistic Injury</span></b></h1><p><span>Fortunately, it does not end with the wound. Or, it does not </span><i><span>have</span></i><span> to. Healing from the wound is possible, natural even, but it requires a particular psychological movement: a kind of letting go, a </span><i><span>turning it into something else</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>In his book </span><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/en/book/show/31487.Unweaving_the_Rainbow"><i><span>Unweaving the Rainbow</span></i></a><span> </span><i><span>(1998)</span></i><span>, Richard Dawkins describes how, for many people, science leads to a kind of </span><i><span>disenchantment.</span></i><span> The title of the book is taken from Keats's poem </span><i><span>Lamia</span></i><span>.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Do not all charms fly</span><br /><span>At the mere touch of cold philosophy?</span><br /><span>There was an awful rainbow once in heaven:</span><br /><span>We know her woof, her texture; she is given</span><br /><span>In the dull catalogue of common things.</span><br /><span>Philosophy will clip an Angel's wings,</span><br /><span>Conquer all mysteries by rule and line,</span><br /><span>Empty the haunted air, and gnomed mine -</span><br /><span>Unweave a rainbow, as it erewhile made</span><br /><span>The tender-person'd Lamia melt into a shade.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>But science (natural philosophy) is not cold, Dawkins argues. No, </span><i><span>quite the opposite</span></i><span>. The rainbow, through Newton, through spectrography, revealing the spectrum folded into the sunlight that hits tiny water droplets, is something at which "the heart of any poet worthy of the title Romantic could not fail to leap up". Charles Darwin makes something akin to this move in the closing sentence of </span><i><span>On the Origin of Species</span></i><span> (1859):</span></p><p><span>"There is grandeur in this view of life, with its several powers, having been originally breathed into a few forms or into one; and that, whilst this planet has gone cycling on according to the fixed law of gravity, from so simple a beginning endless forms most beautiful and most wonderful have been, and are being, evolved."</span></p><p><span>(I really love </span><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/en/book/show/45894068-mama-s-last-hug"><span>Frans de Waal'</span></a><span>s writings, which sometimes make me think that we have still not healed fully from Darwin’s wound...)</span></p><p><span>Right now, our intelligence is where our self-love is attached. Soon we will have to turn that into something else, what it will look like, I do not know. And I notice that every answer I reach for - that we'll find some new source of purpose, that AI itself might help provide it, that maybe we will just “get used to it”, or that a different way of living will emerge - is still, I think, speaking the language of the wound.</span></p><p><span>So rather than attempt an answer, I will leave you with two pictures that I think beautifully illustrate the change the Copernican revolution set in motion: from the center of the universe, to a mote of dust in a sunbeam.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1785970626/lexical_client_uploads/hxhsyt5hexotdzqzhirm.png" /><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmonia_Macrocosmica"><b><span>Andreas Cellarius,</span></b><span> </span><i><b><span>Scenographia Systematis Mundani Ptolemaici</span></b></i><span> </span><b><span>(1660)</span></b></a></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1785969477/lexical_client_uploads/znbsvdz3bx38vegyord7.png" /><p><i><span>Earth as pictured by</span></i><span> </span><a href="https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/science-environment-51491471"><i><span>Voyager</span></i></a><span> </span><i><span>as "a mote of dust suspended in a sunbeam"</span></i><span> (</span><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/en/book/show/61663.Pale_Blue_Dot"><i><span>Pale Blue Dot</span></i></a><span>, 1994)</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JdNjeYC5bk83Kf2Cw/the-fourth-humiliation#comments">Discuss</a>
